Update your preferences in Account Settings As the 25th anniversary of the Sept. 11 terrorist attacks in New York City approaches, Mayor Zohran Mamdani has launched a portal giving the public access to about 170,000 pages of documents concerning air quality, health hazards, and the government’s response following 9/11. Its long-sought documents, which had previously been kept out of the public eye, show that New York City leaders were aware of the dangers posed by toxic contamination around Ground Zero following the attacks, even while they continued to tell New Yorkers that the air was safe to breathe. Some of the pages were reportedly found in 68 boxes stashed in a city office, which advocates argue had been kept away from the public for years. According to a press release from the Mayor's Office, the records "were not located until 2025 despite multiple Freedom of Information Law (FOIL) requests." Another document included in the records is the so-called Harding memo, an October 2001 document showing that city officials had in the weeks after the attacks already anticipated approximately 10,000 legal claims arising from exposure to toxic air. The new portal was announced Tuesday morning at a press conference that was also attended by longtime advocate Jon Stewart and others involved in the yearslong legal effort to obtain the records. In June, the mayor allocated $34.2 million from the city’s $125.8 billion fiscal year 2027 budget to create and maintain the portal. "Now, close to 25 years later, more people have died from 9/11-related illnesses than were killed on the day itself," Mamdani said at the conference announcing the portal. The World Trade Center Health Program, which provides no-cost monitoring and treatment for covered 9/11-related health conditions, said thousands continue to be diagnosed with ailments resulting from their exposure to dust, smoke, debris, and the traumatic events. The city’s Fire Department released a report on Sept. 1 detailing the lasting effects of the 9/11 tragedy on first responders. More than 12,000 FDNY members have been diagnosed and certified with at least one related physical or mental health condition, including cancers, according to the report. The mayor’s office has said that additional documents will be added on a rolling basis. "The City expects substantially more documents will be released in the coming year as they are reviewed, and personally identifiable information is removed," the press release said. “For too long, New Yorkers have had to fight for access to records that should have been available to them in the first place," Mamdani said in the press release. "This portal is part of our administration’s enduring commitment to honor those we lost, care for those living with lasting health consequences and give thousands of New Yorkers the answer they’ve been asking for.” Mamdani also announced Tuesday that the city had settled two lawsuits brought by 9/11 Health Watch, ending a multiyear legal battle over records concerning post-9/11 air quality and health risks. The New York Times obtained early access to the documents and reported that they contain information relating to 15 John Street, near the collapsed World Trade Center, which purportedly detected asbestos—at times exceeding acceptable limits—about a year after the attack. Among the pages was also an audit from November 2001, submitted to the federal Environmental Protection Agency, which reportedly showed high concentrations of benzene, a cancer-causing substance, in the air near the towers, according to the Times. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has said that exposure to these contaminants increases the risk of developing health conditions, such as illnesses concerning the airways and digestive systems. The CDC added that after the attacks, around 400,000 people were exposed to these contaminants and other factors that increased their risk for such conditions. Taking questions at the conference on Tuesday, Mamdani addressed what people can expect to learn from the documents that they might not have already known. "I think that there is going to be a lot to learn from these documents," he said, adding that it's important that "those who faced such sustained pushback for years are finally told by the city of New York: 'You were right.'" The release of the documents coincides with Mamdani drawing calls from families of victims of the tragedy and some Republican politicians not to attend the 9/11 remembrance event in New York City on Friday. In July, 9/11 widower Giovanni Galante began a petition against Mamdani’s attendance, citing concerns over “Mamdani's public positions, associations, and responses to rhetoric that many perceive as hostile.” The petition cites Mamdani’s refusal to condemn those who say “globalize the intifada” as one of the issues. The petition also references Mamdani’s appointment of Ramzi Kassem, who worked for the Biden Administration, as chief counsel to the mayor. Kassem served as legal counsel for Ahmed al-Darbi, who plead guilty to charges stemming from a 2002 al-Qaeda tanker bombing plot and who is the brother-in-law of one of the hijackers in the plane that crashed into the Pentagon. Former New York Gov. George Pataki told a local CBS affiliate on Thursday that Mamdani should first reject Hasan Piker, who previously earned public ire over a comment that the U.S. “deserved” the attack. Mamdani has already denounced Piker’s comment. Rudy Giuliani, who was the mayor of New York in 2001, told right-leaning news organization Newsmax that Mamdani should skip the event. “Having lost friends there and being very close to many of the families, it offends me that he's coming,” Giuliani said. The former mayor also attacked Mamdani’s religion, Islam, and baselessly suggested the mayor was supportive of Muslim extremist ideology. Mamdani, the city’s first Muslim mayor who was nine years old when the attack happened, has long faced Islamophobic comments. Speaking to the Times last year, Mamdani lamented how the 2001 attacks had made anti-Muslim prejudice “a fact of life” for Muslim-Americans, claiming: “It was this horrific day that was also, for many New Yorkers, the moment at which they were marked an ‘other.’” Before Giuliani’s comments, Mamdani signed city executive orders designating Sept. 11 a New York-wide "Day of Remembrance and Service" and updating emergency preparedness procedures. But the signing caught the ire of some critics after Mamdani handed the 9/11 ceremonial pen to Kassem. Despite the pushback, Mamdani still plans to attend the commemoration on Friday. “I’m proud to be the mayor of this city,” Mamdani said. “I love this city. I love this country. And what I want this week to be a focus on is on the families whose loved ones were stolen from them 25 years ago in the horrific act of terror of September 11th, and a focus also on the first responders who gave everything they could to this city and to the people who came together to try and help their fellow New Yorkers.”
The Atlantic
How Breakfast Became Dessert
2026-09-08

How Breakfast Became Dessert

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Breakfast-cereal boxes are a window into both the politics of food and American culture. As a particularly timely example, since Robert F. Kennedy Jr. took office last year as secretary of health and human services, he has called for the removal of artificial dyes from breakfast cereals. On Fox News in 2024, he lamented that the bright colors in Froot Loops are “literally poisoning our kids.” (How exactly these dyes interact with human health is complicated.) Cereal companies responded right away. General Mills announced last month that it had transitioned its entire U.S. portfolio of cereals—including Trix and Lucky Charms—to all-natural dyes made from fruits, veggies, and spices. Kellogg had previously announced plans to do the same by the end of 2027. Target stopped selling any cereals with artificial colors earlier this year. For the Make America Healthy Again movement, the removal of artificial dyes is a big win. But cereal still comes with a major concern for many consumers. Nearly all ready-to-eat breakfast cereals sold in the United States—and especially those marketed to children—are presweetened. The initial loading of breakfast cereals with sugar has been attributed to a single person: Jim Rex, a heating-equipment salesman who lived in Pennsylvania in the early 20th century. As the story goes, Rex was appalled by the spoonfuls of sugar his kids added to their breakfast cereals and set out to create a version that would not induce such behavior. His brilliant contribution: a glossy honey coating on puffed wheat, a cereal he named Ranger Joe Wheat Honnies in 1939. It didn’t take long for Rex’s strategy to backfire. By 1949, Post had launched Sugar Crisp. Kellogg introduced Sugar Pops in 1951, Sugar Frosted Flakes in 1952, and Froot Loops in 1963, each among the company’s all-time best sellers. General Mills launched Sugar Smiles in 1953. The next year, the company added sugar and artificial colors to its Kix cereal, thus creating Trix. In 1950, such presweetened cereals represented just 5 percent of all cereals sold, but by the mid-1970s, kids accounted for almost 40 percent of all cereal consumption. In 2014, the Environmental Working Group estimated that kids could ingest about nine pounds of sugar a year just by eating a serving of cereal every day. Advocates for children’s health have long complained about the high sugar content of breakfast cereals. In the early 1970s, Congress held hearings to highlight cereal’s inadequate nutritional quality. Cereal companies objected, arguing, correctly, that grains are nutritious. But they also responded by trying to make their products appear less sugary. They began by removing the S-word from cereal names, and some substituted honey for sugar on the theory that people perceive honey as more wholesome and nutritious. In the 1990s, companies hit on another clever strategy: If they used multiple sugar types, they could list grains first on a product’s ingredients but sprinkle sugars throughout the list. Cereals looked healthier, but when Consumer Reports compared the sugars in 27 breakfast cereals in 2008, it found that many were still 50 percent sugar by weight. Rumors circulated that federal agencies were working to set nutrition standards for foods advertised on television programs aimed at children younger than 12, which would likely spell trouble for sugary cereals. In 2009, General Mills pledged to reduce sugar in its most popular kids’ cereals—most of which had at least 12 grams a serving—to single digits. Its pledge would apply to 10 cereals, including some of its most popular, such as Lucky Charms and Cocoa Puffs. The results were mixed. By 2012, General Mills had reached the single-digit goal for Cinnamon Toast Crunch, Cookie Crisp, and more. Some of this success was due to decreased serving size. And the best General Mills could do for Lucky Charms was 10 grams of sugar in a three-quarters-cup serving—which the company found to be the minimum sweet spot for kids’ acceptance. The project had encountered a harsh reality: Kids like sugar, and companies remove it at their peril. Sugary cereal is not just a consumer concern; it is also a matter of government nutrition policy. Since 1980, the federal Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants, and Children (WIC) has limited the sugars in cereals it will cover to six grams an ounce, about half of the amount in typical cereals aimed at kids. WIC can be a lucrative business for cereals that qualify, so the limit has induced companies to take two actions. The first is to formulate new cereals with less sugar specifically for the WIC market. In 2006, for example, General Mills introduced a new kid-targeted cereal, Dora the Explorer, “lightly sweetened cinnamon corn puffs,” with six grams of sugar in a 27-gram serving—an amount that meets the WIC standards. The second is to petition to change how sugar in cereals is counted. When the six-gram rule went into effect, in the early 1980s, Kellogg immediately objected. Its Bran Flakes met the WIC standards, but Raisin Bran did not; it contained 12 grams of sugar an ounce, seven from the raisins. Kellogg argued that raisins are healthy and pointed out that WIC was encouraging women to add raisins to their diet to increase their iron intake. For decades, despite Kellogg’s persistent efforts, the U.S. Department of Agriculture did not budge. But times change. In 2024, USDA issued its third comprehensive update to the WIC food package since 1975. The agency retained the six-gram limit but specified that it applied only to added, not total, sugars. It said the policy change was justified to align with listing “added sugars” on food labels and with the government publication Dietary Guidelines for Americans, and to create a “more permissive standard for products containing naturally occurring sugars.” The distinction between natural and added sugars has continued under Kennedy. The new dietary guidelines, released in January, declare that no amount of added sugar is recommended for healthy eating. At a press conference announcing the new guidelines, Kennedy told reporters, “Today, our government declares war on added sugar.” Current breakfast cereals have less sugar than they did years ago, but it’s hard to remove completely. Some amount of sugar is still needed to mask the bitter taste of added vitamins. Sugar also helps cereal flakes, puffs, and Os hold their shape in milk. Less sugar is better for kids’ health, but more is better for structural integrity—and taste. For a truly healthy cereal, look to a time before Jim Rex. The most nutritious option will have one ingredient: wheat or oats. Even if you add your own teaspoon of sugar, it will be only four grams. This article has been adapted from Marion Nestle and Lisa Sutherland’s new book, Sugar Coated. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Raising Independent Kids Shouldn’t Be This Hard
2026-09-08

Raising Independent Kids Shouldn’t Be This Hard

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This past June, a Virginia mother named Karyann Parkinson took a bike ride with her 5-year-old son and stopped by a pond inside their gated community to collect goose feathers from the grass. The two of them rode back home, whereupon Parkinson’s son asked his mother if he could walk back to the pond by himself to fetch a few more feathers. Parkinson, who was eight months pregnant with her fifth child, studying for the bar exam, and—perhaps most pressing—making lunch, agreed. The walk was relatively short, along a sidewalk 10 feet from the road and across two intersections with crosswalks, which Parkinson knew her son could navigate successfully (he had done so six times that morning). But on his way, the boy was spotted by a concerned passerby, who notified a community security guard, who in turn notified the police. Before long, several police officers were at Parkinson’s door, followed by Child Protective Services agents. Officials in the case decided to press charges against Parkinson, and last week she was convicted of contributing to the delinquency of a minor and placed on Virginia’s child-abuse-and-neglect registry, where she will remain for seven years. Parkinson told me that her son—who competently walked the security guard back to their house after politely explaining that he wasn’t allowed to get into cars with strangers—had become distraught upon learning that he had apparently done something very bad. And although the police treated Parkinson as though she were negligent, her husband, who works as a marriage and family therapist and sees a lot of anxious young adults, tried to explain to the officers that they had made a deliberate choice to let their children encounter appropriate levels of challenge and risk. But the officials remained unmoved. Two weeks later, two police officers showed up at Parkinson’s door with a warrant for her arrest. “It just kept escalating,” she told me. “No one was taking a step back and going, Oh, this is fine. This is a fine situation.” She gave birth to her baby a week later. Last week’s guilty verdict, Parkinson said, has been disorienting. Having spent 15 years taking some pride in her mindful choices as a parent, she has lately been doubting herself—“and thinking, Am I really crazy?” Her lawyers have appealed the decision, and Parkinson will receive a new trial. People are having fewer children around the world for a variety of reasons, but some observers suggest that the steep expectations placed on parents—and mothers in particular—may be at least partly to blame for the fertility cliff in the United States. In response to a pervasive atmosphere of parental stress, a number of social-media influencers and public intellectuals have recommended that parents just do less—no more assiduously planned birthday extravaganzas, no more enrichment programs scheduled from breakfast to bedtime, no more hawkish monitoring to rescue children from any hint of danger or discomfort. But even though working parents may long for the days when children were more independent—a wish reinforced by countless editorials declaring the wisdom of letting children roam freely, away from their screens—society seems to have irrevocably changed. Most families now include two parents working full-time, and although many of these adults enjoyed the unchaperoned pleasures of a latchkey existence, the sight of children left to their own devices now horrifies not only the neighbors but also cops and prosecutors. In other words, parents are caught in a vise made up of financial demands and socially inflated expectations around parenting. Parkinson’s story is not unique. In 2008, Lenore Skenazy made national news when she published a column about letting her 9-year-old son ride the subway home after a trip to Bloomingdale’s in Midtown Manhattan, a decision that many people likewise characterized as a form of child abuse or neglect. Skenazy, who has since founded what she calls the Free-Range Kids movement, occasionally publishes stories about mothers who are arrested or locked up for letting their children enjoy what used to be considered ordinary childhood pleasures. In 2020, a Georgia mother of five was jailed for allowing her 14-year-old daughter to babysit her younger siblings during the pandemic, when schools and day-care facilities were closed but parents still had to work. In 2024, a mother of four in Georgia was handcuffed when her 10-year-old son was found walking into town, less than a mile from their home, while she was taking one of her other children to a doctor’s appointment. Parkinson’s story has become a national phenomenon in large part because Skenazy first called attention to it in Reason magazine. Raising children comes with enough trade-offs without enforcing unforgiving standards for parental attention, entertainment, and supervision. Notably, none of the cases in which police arrested mothers for their presumed negligence involved children who had been actually harmed. One hesitates to admit to such a taboo practice in print, but my own 10-year-old daughter has ventured out unaccompanied to hang out with her friends or pick up a few things from the store, and although I have fretted over getting pinched by the police, she treasures those opportunities to establish some independence even more than I appreciate time away from monitoring and enabling her various whims and desires. Mixing in activities that don’t require constant parental oversight is unfortunately a daring practice these days, but it seems crucial to balancing work and other adult responsibilities with the demands of raising children. This is not to say that allowing kids some independence comes without risks: Cars pose a threat, as does every child’s latent desire to engage in mischief. Unknown adults assailing children with ill intentions, though a terrifying prospect, is exceedingly rare; stranger abductions account for less than 1 percent of missing-child cases nationwide. But these risks may nevertheless be overstated in our social imagination because ours is a low-trust society; in other countries, young people are granted much more freedom because parents presume that the adults they encounter will be helpful and supportive. What Parkinson, Skenazy, and many other parents understand is that independence is good for children, who stand to build confidence and competence by doing things on their own. And fostering that independence is, in the end, the primary goal of parenting. The job is hard and bittersweet by design: Every step they take in the world is a step away from you. This, of course, is where their journeys are destined to lead.

Lonnie Bunch Is Leaving the Smithsonian

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie Bunch plans to announce his retirement from the institution at the end of this year, bringing to a close a tenure marked by unprecedented pressure on the museum complex from a White House keen on controlling how to tell the nation’s history. A person with knowledge of the situation confirmed the departure to The Atlantic. Bunch’s exit will leave the Smithsonian, which is partially federally funded, with a vacuum at the top as it faces escalating efforts from the Trump administration to influence its work. Bunch, known to be diplomatic and evenhanded, had largely staved off the White House’s encroachment over a tumultuous 18 months in which the Trump administration issued executive orders and memos criticizing the museums and threatened to withhold funding. Most recently, President Trump signed an executive order calling for signage to be added to the exterior of the National Museum of American History warning about what the administration believes is an inaccurate and partisan telling of history. Before becoming secretary, Bunch was best known as the founding director of the National Museum of African American History and Culture. This is a developing story and will be updated.

The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye
2026-09-08

The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The human brain has a method for dealing with the intrinsic fear of death. Terror-management theory, which builds on the work of the cultural anthropologist Ernest Becker, explains that the psyche handles this fear in a variety of ways. Many people either escape reality or adopt a belief system that promises immortality: so on the one hand, sex, drugs, or entertainment; on the other, religion, fame, or careerism. Or we might immerse ourselves in family, or culture—something that imbues life with meaning. Otherwise, the theory goes, we would be cognitively immobilized by impending doom. In her gut-twisting third novel, Switzy, Emma Cline does not abide by the tenets of terror management. Instead, she pulls death out from behind the curtain and forces her readers to look it in the eye for more than 300 pages. Switzy follows the final few days in the life of David Hastings, a former high-flying executive who’s been forced into retirement, and obsolescence, by an Alzheimer’s diagnosis. He has taken advantage of Switzerland’s right-to-die laws, paying the fees (a paltry sum for a wealthy man) and filling out the required paperwork. Now he’ll swallow some barbiturates and die in an impersonal room just outside Zurich, far from his family and his home in America. Cline’s approach is merciless—not toward David, for whom she shows tenderness and grace, but toward the reader, who will be nearly flattened by this novel. Or so I sincerely hope, because death ought to flatten us once in a while. Switzy begins mid-flight, as David hurtles across the Atlantic in a friend’s private jet. Accompanied by Cody—a former corporate underling who now functions as David’s caretaker, body man, and secretary—he lands first in London. There, he has dinner with his only child, an emotionally distant daughter who doesn’t know about her father’s decline, let alone his upcoming euthanasia. David tours a museum, visits his grandson, reboards the plane for his final flight, to Zurich; he dazedly lets Cody steer him around as a parent would guide a toddler. The younger man negotiates the intricacies of the world on David’s behalf, buffering him from the demands of day-to-day life—buying a museum ticket, sending a text message, procuring beverages. David needs the help: He is at times absent in his own mind. As rendered by Cline in a wandering, dreamlike style, this becomes a startling and eerie phenomenon—like the moment you realize you’ve been driving for miles but don’t recall anything about the journey. Far from experiencing the blissful ignorance that some people might project onto dementia patients, David is all too aware that his brain has locked away basic memories and social customs. He writes his grandson’s name, Rainer, in a notebook so he can recall it; a clock “in big kindergarten letters” informs him of the date and time each morning; he stares too long at someone in the museum and provokes his annoyance. And he sees each gap, knows that it signals another loss. “Work,” he thinks as he chews a croissant, “everything seemed like work. Even these good things—even this buttered mouthful.” Read: A different way to think about medicine’s most stubborn enigma The first half of Switzy crawls by, but it feels achingly necessary. This is time the way David experiences it, slowed down and sometimes skipped over: Alzheimer’s novels are rare, and those told from the patient’s point of view are even rarer. Cline, always a graceful stylist, employs a kind of abbreviated modernism to leap among David’s thoughts. They skitter backwards to memories of his failed marriage and his time at a boys’ boarding school. They run in circles, around catchy sayings—such as a mnemonic about Henry VIII’s abbreviated marriages, “Divorced, beheaded, died; divorced, beheaded, survived”—that Alzheimer’s hasn’t stolen from him. And they jump ahead to the moment and manner of his death, which inches closer with every turned page. Cline’s last novel, The Guest, also followed a character who hastily approaches a reckoning. Alex, a poor and wily 22-year-old sex worker who has just been kicked out of the home of her most recent catch, Simon, has to make it through five days in the Hamptons, until she can plead to be let back into the high-end milieu of Simon’s Labor Day party. Dead-eyed and mutable, Alex, like David, is trying to put on just enough of a show to pass muster with the people around her. For her, a different kind of death awaits—complete impoverishment, and removal from a perch where she may or may not belong. Switzy raises the stakes on this kind of quest. As the novel’s second half picks up steam, so does the cadence of David’s confrontations with the fact of his looming extinction. When the plane lands in Zurich, an official comes on to inspect their passports. “David smiled to himself. It was obvious, wasn’t it? This man was Death. Why else would he know to come right onboard?” Soon after, he sees Death again as a waiter, a busboy, two restaurant patrons. On his last night, he wakes repeatedly in his hotel, checking the sky’s brightness as a sign of how much time he has left. An Omega watch advertisement catches his eye, and though he doesn’t say it, the meaning is clear: Omega is the last letter in the Greek alphabet, the universal symbol of finality. As long as David cannot stop returning to his ticking clock, neither can you. This kind of sustained attention to the one topic many people spend their lives avoiding is practically subversive—a thrill, really. And it’s possibly a public service too. Studies have repeatedly shown that people who think about death on a regular basis are happier, because contemplating mortality reinforces the idea, as Emily Dickinson put it, “That it will never come again/ Is what makes life so sweet.” I daresay most readers won’t feel much happiness reading Switzy—though it does have glimmers of humor—but they might experience moments of sublime clarity. Death functions here in a way that isn’t common in fiction. Its presence doesn’t make this novel a tearjerker—it’s not, for example, Maggie O’Farrell’s Hamnet, which beautifully laments the loss of a child. It offers no spectacle akin to Anne Boleyn’s trembling march to the scaffold at the end of Hilary Mantel’s singular historical novel, Bring Up the Bodies. Unlike many 19th-century novels, Switzy doesn’t use death to solve a social problem, à la Anna Karenina (romantic devastation) and Madame Bovary (financial ruin). Nor is death here a handy plot device, as it is in so many works across the past century. Switzy is not about the living who are left behind. Cline’s approach to death marks different territory: It’s about raw, undeniable truth. Read: Charles Manson, the girls, and the banality of desire Dying is a transformative event: People pass from the only reality they’ve ever been able to recognize into something nebulous and ungraspable (even for the most fervent religious believers). And this might sound obvious, but you, reader, will die. So will I. Much of humanity has raised its defenses high against death, perhaps unintentionally, as Ernest Becker proposes. So how will it feel to see a man marching straight toward it—not unflinchingly, or tremulously, but with a perfectly ordinary mixture of confusion and acceptance? In Switzy’s final pages, David becomes a man who cannot help but be alive, even in his last moments. He doesn’t want to eat breakfast, but he feels it’s “better to have something in the stomach.” He feels irritated that he is given a blue pen to sign documents—“blue ink was for grocery lists.” He fumbles over his last words. Cline writes, “His was both the realest life in the room and the most unreal.” The line between everything and nothing is as thin as a breath of air. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

The Myth of Perfect Knowledge
2026-09-08

The Myth of Perfect Knowledge

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The modern world owes a lot to the Enlightenment, the 18th-century intellectual movement that emphasized the power of reason. But the Enlightenment also created a myth: that we can get to know the objective world completely, with total clarity, as if we were looking at it from the outside. This myth is challenged by the Enlightenment’s greatest legacy—modern science. Reality is more complex than we expected. Physics—my trade, and perhaps the most objective among the sciences—questions the possibility of a full description of how things objectively are. An electron can be in a “quantum superposition,” so that to an observer, it doesn’t occupy just one location—and so can you, my reader. This does not mean that you could perceive being dislocated between two positions; as perceived by yourself, you are always in just one place. But an object’s properties may differ depending on who, or what, is looking at them—or may not even be defined at all. Properties are not simple features of things: They are relations between one thing and another. What we call “the world” is therefore only how some parts of reality manifest themselves to other parts—us, for instance. Humanity has always been aware that our understanding is partial and biased. But the fact that an objective and knowable reality is incompatible with the grammar of physics is astonishing. The Enlightenment could consider the mind as being outside the world, in an abstract realm of reason where knowledge resides. But human minds are not outside reality, watching it from its exterior. We, like everything else, are part of the very nature we study. Our knowledge is only one example of the many correlations that intertwine different parts of nature. In the early 20th century, Niels Bohr, a founder of quantum theory, realized that quantum phenomena indicate that we cannot discard our own physical nature when we observe the world. The ideal of grasping objective reality by making the observer irrelevant is incompatible with physics. We are quantum processes interacting with other quantum processes. Our knowledge is a rich but incomplete set of correlations between one piece of the world–ourselves–and the rest of it. Adam Frank: The truth physics can no longer ignore To see this fact from another point of view, consider saying “It’s raining and Ann does not know it.” This makes sense: You know that it’s raining and that Ann doesn’t. But if you replace “Ann” with a first-person pronoun, the result is odd: “It’s raining and I do not know it.” How can you declare that it’s raining if you do not know it? This observation points to the fact that every proposition can only express the knowledge of the speaker or writer. No proposition is asserted from outside reality, because there is nothing outside reality. To say “It is raining” is really to say: “I, as part of reality, know that it’s raining.” Objective statements independent of any knower are fictions. Purely objective reality is a useful myth, but only a myth. Anything said about the state of the world comes from someone’s singular perspective. This means that all of our knowledge is intrinsically uncertain. All statements have unavoidable margins of uncertainty. Indeed, we can be wrong about anything, even the basic facts of our own lives. My father grew up sure that his first name was Franco until he asked for a copy of his birth certificate and found out that my grandfather, without telling my grandma, had registered him as Giulio. He had been wrong about his own name. Humanity has been collectively wrong over and over again. We thought the sun moved around the earth, then learned that it is the earth that orbits the sun. Great scientists are even more likely to be wrong than others, because they have the courage to try—Einstein’s published work includes many mistakes. But in science, as in daily life, we do not need certainty; being reasonably sure is good enough. If I have an airplane to catch, I will go to the airport even though I’m not totally sure that the flight will take off; if I waited for certainty, I’d never leave home. Modern science offers an enormous body of knowledge that is effective and, in many domains, far better than all the alternatives, but it gives us no absolute certainty, no ultimate story, no Truth with a capital T. Within science, there are different ways of describing the same things. A glass of water can be described in terms of physics (its mass, velocity), thermodynamics (its temperature), chemistry (there is more than just H2O in any glass of water), biology (bacteria swim in it), particle physics (its electrons, protons, and neutrons), psychology (“Why did I fill the glass?”), economics (its price), and more. Each account is valid in its own terms; each reflects a way in which the same object interacts with different parts of reality, from atoms to the global economy. But none exhausts an “ultimate truth” of the glass. The same is true for human beings. I can think of you, my reader, as a body, as a mind, or as a soul. These aren’t different entities: They are the same entity, described in different languages, using different conceptual schemes, focusing on different aspects of the same you and different ways in which you interact with other parts of reality. There is no sharp separation or metaphysical gap between body and soul; matter and spirit; the physical world and the world of laws, ideas, and norms. In this sense, all things are equal: They are all aspects of the same natural world, considered from different perspectives, none of which is the ultimate one. Everything can be considered fundamental from one perspective or another. The expression “the equality of all things” comes from the title of the second chapter of The Book of Zhuangzi, one of the greatest books of humankind—an ancient classic of Chinese philosophy that articulates a similar intriguing perspectival and anti-foundational naturalism. This is the view to which I think modern science leads us. Many people resist it, some out of fear of losing solid reference points: objectivity, certainties, absolute morality, an immortal soul. But scientific knowledge does not become less valuable if you remain open to doubt. Morality does not lose power if you recognize that it is rooted in our biology, culture, and history. Our values do not weaken because they are rooted in what we are, not in something outside us. And life is even more beautiful and precious without dreams of immortality. We are not—as French existentialism described us—thrown into alien nature. In nature, we are home. In fact, it seems to me that recognizing the perspectival aspect of knowledge, values, and beliefs, and of reality itself, could help us become a bit less arrogant, a bit humbler and wiser. Conflicts between individuals or countries don’t stem only from greed and thirst for power. They are also fueled by the belief that we are objectively right and the enemy is objectively wrong, which ignores the plurality of perspectives. We have our values that guide us, but understanding the perspective of others is the first step toward wisdom—and toward peace. In our era of nuclear armaments, ignoring that others have different perspectives, and failing to respect them, could drag the world into a catastrophe.
The Atlantic
Zoos Are Doubling Down on Very Unnatural Habitats
2026-09-08

Zoos Are Doubling Down on Very Unnatural Habitats

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Hudson, a 10-year-old arctic fox at Massachusetts’s Stone Zoo, was not doing all that much when his caretakers came by his enclosure one sunny morning last month. But the moment he detected their approach, he scurried over. His eyes focused on the pink, kibble-filled puzzle box they were holding—a programmable, lab-made device that’s part of an experiment in letting captive animals play around with technology. In the past year, in partnership with a team led by Rébecca Kleinberger, an engineer and a computer scientist at Northeastern University, Stone Zoo has introduced Hudson to his puzzle feeder, two black bears to a motion-sensitive bubble machine, a family of colobus monkeys to an interactive cabinet of mirrors, and more. The zoo is not alone in its tech curiosity. At the Maryland Zoo, caretakers have debuted a hunting simulation for polar bears, in which a robot arm extends and retracts a romaine leaf smeared with peanut butter—mimicking a prey animal poking its head out of an ice hole. At Zoo Atlanta, keepers have partnered with engineers at the Georgia Institute of Technology to develop an interactive audio wall where elephants can insert their trunks into motion-sensitive holes to trigger a variety of low-frequency sounds. At some facilities, penguins have been given access to iPad games designed for pet cats, and orangutans have been taught to play on an Xbox. Hudson the arctic fox makes a case for the benefits that captive animals can derive from tech. His quarters at the zoo, where he lives alone, offer few of the thrills he’d experience in the wild—chasing after live rodents and birds, wooing mates, fleeing wolves and bears. In the past, when I’d come to his enclosure as a visitor to the zoo, he had always been snoozing; simpler toys rarely captured his attention for more than a few minutes at a time, his keepers told me. This time, he trotted. He sniffed. He wriggled his tail. He reared up on his hind legs and used his long-clawed front paws to spin the pink wheel of his puzzle counterclockwise, then clockwise, until it yielded crunchy bits of kibble. When Kleinberger’s team first approached Pete Costello, the assistant curator at Stone Zoo, and his colleagues about the prospect of introducing the zoo’s residents to technology, his mind swam with images of creatures clutching iPads, doomscrolling their days away, he told me. But as Hudson spun his box—which can be toggled up and down through four levels of difficulty and programmed to release food at set intervals—I glanced over at Costello and the other keepers. They were beaming like proud parents and complimenting the fox’s deft moves: Ooh, good spin … Yup, he’s got it right away! Regardless of how well wild animals are cared for in captivity, their lives by definition shrink. In enclosures, removed from nature’s shifting circumstances and perils, and, often, the company of their own kind, they “lead very impoverished lives,” Clara Mancini, a pioneering researcher in the field of animal-computer interaction at the Open University, in the United Kingdom, told me. Technology may at first seem to make captive animals’ lives more artificial. But it’s starting to look like a good way to coax them into behaving more wildly. The field of technological enrichment for animals might have taken off decades earlier, Joey Golden, the curator of animal-behavior programs at the Maryland Zoo, told me. In the 1970s and ’80s, for instance, the scientist Hal Markowitz tinkered with devices that taught Diana monkeys to dispense food from a vending machine, or flung “flying meatballs” for servals. The animals were, by all appearances, interested in Markowitz’s devices. But “what he was building,” Golden said, “was incredibly artificial” to others in the research community who were focused on better replicating wild habitats. Animal-behavior researchers have since come around to the idea that the aesthetics of an animal’s surroundings are less important than how well those surroundings serve them. Creatures need the unpredictability of the natural world; in the monotony of captivity, where humans are in charge, a device that can shift with an animal’s needs can offer that creature “a little more control,” Kleinberger told me. A wobbly chew toy stuffed with a mini meatball might entertain a fox like Hudson for a few minutes. But it can’t operate on a shifting schedule or become more difficult as the user learns its quirks—unlike a teched-up puzzle feeder. Kleinberger got her start by creating voice-technology tools for musicians and people who stutter. But several years ago, keepers at the San Diego Zoo asked if she would develop a device to help a beats-loving hyacinth macaw named Sampson control his own playlist. Kleinberger was pleased to see not only that the device not only gave Sampson access to the tunes he wanted to hear, but that he’d bob his head along with the music to attract the types of zoo visitors whom he seemed to particularly enjoy. “He really liked children or people wearing red T-shirts,” Kleinberger said. Sampson, in effect, had made the technology his own. Determining what animals may get out of different technologies requires some guesses and leaps. At Stone Zoo, a pair of black bears, Smoky and Bubba, are certainly interested in the device that Kleinberger’s team built for them—a motion-sensitive machine that blows scented bubbles. The day I visited, both bears lumbered over to the lemon-scented, then bacon-scented bubbles streaming into their enclosure; they took big, huffing whiffs; flicked their ears; and occasionally licked their chops. The bubbles were stimulating the bears’ keen noses, engaging a sense they might not otherwise use very often. But, of course, the bears couldn’t tell us how much they actually liked the experience. When I asked Mancini how researchers know the full effects that technology has on other creatures, her answer was simple: “We don’t.” Animal behaviorists generally don’t like to use words such as enjoyment and boredom to describe their study subjects. “Those are very human concepts,” Kleinberger said. But if the goal is to improve captive animals’ lives, sentiments at least akin to enjoyment and boredom are what enrichment researchers must try to measure. Scientists are left to use the animals’ actions as a code, Malu Celli, Zoo New England’s vice president of animal care, told me. Researchers look for certain behaviors that they might feel are lacking in captivity: more swimming by a penguin, more walking by a bear, more foraging by a fox. They’ll also pay attention to how a creature’s senses are engaged—whether an animal’s ears lift or its eyes focus on a machine—while watching for subtle signs of distress. A positive result can be as simple as “Do they come back to it?” Kleinberger said. Or, presented with the same system, do they run away? Engagement doesn’t necessarily mean fulfillment. An animal that’s spending long periods of time with a screen might just be drawn in by the lights. And in the same way that chasing a laser might infuriate a cat who can never catch its blinking red “prey,” touch screens that ask animals to chase virtual objects might never satisfy predatory instincts. Tech might also suck up so much of an animal’s attention that it doesn’t do much of anything else, or engross a group of creatures so much that they begin to compete for access, Marcela Benítez, an animal-behavior researcher at Emory University, told me. At some zoos, in fact, caretakers have entirely banned smartphones around their great-ape enclosures out of concern that the animals might get too much screen time. Researchers are still trying to understand the extent to which different species can recognize two-dimensional images or videos of themselves or others of their own kind. Certain brainy creatures can be good candidates for screen-based interactions: Kleinberger and her colleagues have had success with a video-chatting system for pet parrots, highly social birds that commonly end up isolated in a human home. Many of the parrots they studied would chatter or sing loudly to each other through the screen. One parrot in the study, Kleinberger said, started flying more often after a series of calls with another bird that frequently flitted about. Some of the parrots, though, just lay down next to the device or fell asleep. Several of the scientists I spoke with for this story also happened to be parents of very young children; more than once, they compared the types of enrichment they were tinkering with to the games and toys they’d shared with their toddlers, for better or for worse. Some wondered about the potential for addiction, or even about skewing certain creatures’ perception of reality by overloading them with artifice. But projecting too much of humans’ own complicated relationships with technology onto animals can unnecessarily limit research. Just as animals might derive the same benefits that people do, they also might not experience the same harms. With Hudson, Kleinberger at first worried that the fox might become unhealthily obsessed with his puzzle box. But the presence of the device, she said, instead seemed to boost his enthusiasm for activity in general. It’s hard to say for sure, but maybe the technology reminded him that there was still fun to be had inside his artificial world.

Well technically speaking rust conducts reasonably well (much less than iron of course), but one of the problem is that rust is larger than iron. It pushes apart the material, creates tears, and that is what reduces conductivity. ... as the year that the attack of the robots was stopped by rust. How does AI do with Rust? It seems like most of the AI generated code is in HTML, Typescript, and Python. Is it capable of advanced work in Rust? LLMs focusing on code generation can generate code in nearly any language. I for example use them sometimes for the initial "frame" of Dart/Flutter languages. I worked a while with an unknown LLM (unknown, as it was a research project and the LLMs involved did not disclose their names), that LLM claimed it only needs to see the grammar or examples of a private language I use. As I never bothered to write a formal grammar for it, I gave it a few thousand lines of code. Then it started asking a few questions, and then it was able to produce code in it. My language is based on Lox: https://craftinginterpreters.c... [craftinginterpreters.com] I understand. It's true most AIs can generate code in virtually any language. But, my experience has been that the code quality drops precipitously if the language is not one of the ones I've mentioned and as the code base grows. SQL is great. C and C# not so great. Powershell, surprisingly bad after about 50 or more lines. python and js is great. I think rust is fine, because the language has some hard rules, which are easy to follow and in case the AI does not follow them the compiler gives a hard error. AIs can iterate quite well on hard errors, the weakness is subtle mistakes that can't be caught by compilers, linters, etc. We have used LLM's to generate a lot of Rust very successfully. I would rather trust AI generating Rust than almost any other language because the Rust compiler is so fussy about types and object lifetimes that it gives the LLM far less opportunity to sneaky silly, hard to find, errors in. Also Rust's error messages are very comprehensive and helpful, which LLM's make good use of. This is an incredibly frustrating time to try to use a GPU in general. On Windows, both drivers are shit. Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend
2026-09-08

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It is a mystery why President Trump continues to rely on the hapless team of Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—his son-in-law and fellow real-estate tycoon, respectively—to hammer out treaties to end the most destructive wars of our time: Russia vs. Ukraine and Israel vs. the Palestinians. Yes, Trump doesn’t trust the professional diplomatic corps or experts of any kind. But, as a self-proclaimed businessman, he must notice when aides don’t get the job done, and the Kushner-Witkoff duo have not lured the combatants one step closer to peace; in fact, they’ve arguably intensified the war in Ukraine. Their utter incompetence was on full display over the weekend, as they met with Vladimir Putin in Moscow, then—in their first trip to the Ukrainian capital—Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv. They carried what Trump touted ahead of time as new peace proposals, and concluded both sets of talks with boilerplate adjectives (“productive,” “meaningful,” “positive,” “businesslike”). But in fact, they came away with zilch—and, once again, proved themselves far short of the task. This should come as no surprise. Neither has any diplomatic experience; neither knows anything about Russia or Ukraine or military strategy; nor do they travel with any specialists who might advise them along the way. Witkoff kicked off the tour with an act of full-bore genuflection toward Putin, greeting the Kremlin dictator as follows: First, Mr. President, thank you so much for having us. We were sitting outside just now, me, Jared, and Kirill [Dmitriev], and then Yuri [Ushakov] joined us, and we were all talking about when we retire, we’ll have all these incredible stories and incredible memories, and yours will be right at the top of it all. In other words, Mr. President, he might have added, we come to praise you, not pressure you into taking any actions that you don’t feel like taking. Later in the day, the aforementioned Ushakov, Putin’s senior diplomat, released a summary of the Kremlin team’s position at the meeting: The Russian army has had battlefield successes, meaning it might still win the war; Ukrainian troops must withdraw from the eastern Donbas regions; the war’s “root cause” is Zelensky’s belief that Ukraine is an independent nation; and peace will bring many business opportunities between Russia and the United States. In other words, the usual propaganda. (The U.S. envoys did not release an official “readout” of the meeting, perhaps because they are not officials. That’s right: Kushner and Witkoff hold no government posts, have not been confirmed by the Senate, and are unaccountable to anyone but Trump.) The duo then flew to Poland and from there proceeded to Kyiv by train. (Zelensky had promised not to bomb Moscow while the two were in the Kremlin, but Putin offered no such pledge in return, so the U.S. security team felt Kyiv’s airport was unsafe. In any case, Putin resumed bombarding Ukraine’s capital city shortly after the two departed. Such a good friend.) After this meeting, Zelensky insisted that the three hold a press conference, mainly with Ukrainian reporters. It did not go well. Asked if he felt “ashamed” to meet at the same table with an international war criminal like Putin, Witkoff replied: We’ve always been treated respectfully by President Putin and his team. Relationships are critical in terms of solving conflicts like this. My job, and Jared’s job, is to bring the parties together. All three sentences reflect, and reaffirm, Witkoff’s utter naivete. They have not been treated respectfully; hence the refusal to issue a hands-off order on Kyiv’s airport while the two were in town. Relationships are not critical in solving conflicts; managing interests, either through diplomacy or force, is key. (Trump, too, doesn’t understand this.) Finally, if Witkoff thinks his job is simply to bring the parties together, he doesn’t understand his job. Kushner repeated much of Witkoff’s nonsense, saying that the point of negotiations is to produce a “win-win,” including some sort of favor that fulfills Russia’s “aspirations.” Since Russia’s chief aspirations—as Putin himself has declared—are to obliterate Ukraine as an independent nation and to restore much of the old Russian Empire, this isn’t likely to be the basis for an equitable peace. Along the same lines, Kushner expressed hope that the U.S., Russia, and Ukraine could resume the trilateral talks that began shortly before Trump got sidetracked by the war on Iran (though Kushner didn’t express the chronology that way). The few talks that took place were, as he recalled, such jolly affairs. “Everyone was speaking in Russian to each other. We were discussing these issues in a very extra-friendly manner, all in Russian.” If he’d been escorted by a specialist, Kushner might have been informed that speaking Russian doesn’t make Ukrainians feel merry and welcome, especially with Russian officials who want to be their overlords. (Many Ukrainians these days regard the Russian language, including classic Russian literature, as inherently imperialist.) Another Kushner faux pas, this one perhaps reflecting his ignorance of European history: “There is no such thing in the world as forever enemies or forever allies.” He said this in the context of urging Ukrainians to imagine that they might once again be friendly with Russians. However, the analogy that some observers thought he was invoking—the U.S. alliance with Germany after World War II—was not at all fitting, as it developed only after the U.S. and other allies defeated Nazi Germany, deposed its government, tried many of its leaders for war crimes, and imposed a new constitution. Even then, it wasn’t until 10 years after the war ended that the new Federal Republic of Germany was trusted enough for the allies to invite it to join NATO. Clearly, Kushner wasn’t proposing that Putin be killed or that his government be transformed—though many in the audience might have hoped that he would. It has never been entirely clear how Trump would like this war to end—or if he thinks it should end, or whether he cares one way or the other. Until he decides, keeping Kushner and Witkoff on the case is a waste of jet fuel and time. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.
2026-09-08

Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This is the second in a series of articles, “Try, Try Again?,” drawn from my new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case For (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. The Constitution created a federal republic consisting of states with equal legal status. Under the 10th Amendment, Congress and the executive branch have only limited authority to intrude on the sovereign prerogatives of the states. Individual residents of those states are guaranteed equal protection of the laws. These are pillars of the American constitutional order. To subvert any of them is to subvert the Constitution itself, a subversion that, as the Framers insisted, is impeachable. If a president engages in a pattern of conduct that subordinates the rights and interests of individual states to his personal or partisan political interests, that would be impeachable. Likewise, if a president engages in a pattern of conduct that denies equal protection of the laws of the United States to the citizens of individual states in order to promote his personal or partisan political interests, that, too, would be impeachable. In his second term as president, Donald Trump has continuously done both. To understand how Trump has abused his power against the states, it’s important to understand the power the federal government—and the president, specifically—does have. For example, the widely varying geographic, demographic, social, and economic circumstances of the states has meant that they need different things from the central government. Thus, the unruly interaction of democratic politics with national government accepts a good deal of horse trading over which states will get various types of federal largesse and regulation, along with a degree of more or less constant disagreement between state and federal authorities over policy and resource allocation. In extreme cases, the federal government may employ coercion against states that are sufficiently out of harmony with national law and policy. If a state government openly violates federal law despite clear orders from the federal judiciary—as was the case of some Southern states during the civil rights conflicts of the 20th century—the national government may use appropriate measures, including force, to compel compliance with the law. This is not the current circumstance. Critically, the federal government has to enforce such measures itself rather than commandeering the states to do so. Congress lacks “the power to issue orders directly to the States.” It cannot compel states to issue regulations. It cannot command state officers to participate in or enforce federal laws under what is known as the anti-commandeering doctrine. While Congress can condition a state’s receipt of federal funds on compliance with specified conditions, there are limitations to this power. Congress’ conditions to states must be clear and unambiguous and must have some relationship to the purposes of the federal grants in question. The conditions cannot be imposed without a state’s knowing and voluntary consent. This means that Congress may not set conditions on dispersal of federal funds that effectively offer the state no realistic choice except compliance. While Congress has some authority to induce states to participate in federal initiatives, the president acting on his own authority has no such power. To be sure, the president and the executive branch are charged with lawfully implementing congressional directives about the dispersal of appropriated funds. But the president cannot discriminate between states in dispersing federal funds by imposing his own rules, policy stipulations, or personal preferences, particularly if they contradict or are unrelated to congressional funding directives. Moreover, the post–Civil War 14th Amendment made explicit that the individual inhabitants of states are entitled to equal treatment under national law, which means that people living in different states cannot be treated differently simply because one lives in a state politically aligned with the president and another does not. Trump has flagrantly violated these principles on multiple occasions during his second term. The first violation began on his first day back in office when he issued an executive order commanding that “sanctuary jurisdictions,” meaning states, counties, or municipalities that did not order their law-enforcement authorities to cooperate fully with Trump’s immigration-enforcement actions, “do not receive access to Federal funds.” The initial executive order was followed by two others with the same thrust. These orders are not based on any congressional enactment conditioning federal funding on state or local cooperation with federal immigration efforts. Trump renewed the threat to all federal funding in a statement in January 2026. Following Trump’s executive orders, various elements of the executive branch began to withhold funding to state, regional, and local governments deemed insufficiently compliant with, or openly resistant to, Trump’s immigration program. A number of cities and states that suffered funding cuts sued. At least one court has ruled the cuts illegal. Multiple cases are pending. It is relevant to any article of impeachment premised wholly or in part on coercion of the states over immigration policy that Trump tried essentially the same tactic in his first administration and was blocked by the federal courts. In short, the courts previously ruled that Trump impinged on the sovereignty of the states and ignored Congress’ power of the purse, but he came right back and did it again. This sort of repeat-offender behavior elevates what might otherwise be considered a simple policy or legal dispute into an impeachable offense. As troublesome as Trump’s extortion of state cooperation with his immigration efforts may be, it is at least connected to an articulable policy objective, however controversial. Two other types of denial of federal funds, grants, or programs are even more concerning, and even more compelling as grounds for impeachment. First, the Trump administration has actively discriminated against “blue” states by systemically withholding federal funding, grants, and programmatic assistance. The discrimination is self-evident from the available data. A review of presidential disaster declarations entitling states and localities to federal aid revealed that in Trump’s first 14 months in office, he approved a mere 23 percent of applications from states with a Democratic governor and two Democratic senators, while approving 89 percent of requests from states with a Republican governor and two Republican senators. Moreover, approvals of disaster declarations in Democratic states took twice as long as those to Republican states (80 days vs. 40 days). No pattern remotely similar has ever been observed for past presidents. Moreover, one need not rely on inferences from data. In the fall of 2025, Russell Vought, head of the Office of Management and Budget, announced cancellation of “nearly $8 billion” in grants for clean-energy programs authorized by Congress during the Biden administration—what Vought called the “Green New Scam.” The first indication that these cancellations, in addition to being open violations of Congress’ power of the purse, were based primarily on politics rather than climate policy was that he listed the states in which grants were canceled—and they included no states dominated by Republicans and instead included 16 of the 19 states that had voted for Kamala Harris in the 2024 election. Multiple states, a coalition including energy and environmental organizations and the city of St. Paul, Minnesota, a group of University of California researchers, and others sued the Trump administration over the cancellations. In pleadings filed in the researchers’ case, the U.S. Department of Energy formally stipulated that none of the 284 grants terminated in October 2025 were terminated “based on any programmatic, statutory, cost-reduction or performance-based factor.” The stipulation admitted that, “with one exception, the 284 terminated grants had a recipient location and/or at least one place of performance in a state that awarded its electoral votes to Kamala Harris in the 2024 election and has two Democratic-caucusing senators.” The stipulation concluded by confessing: DOE accepts that the inclusion of grants in the October notice tranche was based solely on the political identity of the grant recipient’s state. … DOE accepts that the differential treatment resulting in the October 2025 termination of Blue State grants and the non-termination of non-Blue State grants was not based on a rational connection between the recipient’s location and/or place of performance and DOE’s past or current agency priorities. As reported by the New York Times, at the same time as the administration was cancelling grants to “blue states,” it “left untouched hundreds of additional energy grants in states that were represented by Republicans and had backed Trump in the last election.” In short, the Trump administration has admitted using payment or withholding of congressionally appropriated funds to punish or retaliate against states and their inhabitants because, and only because, those states support the president’s political opposition. Second, Trump has gone beyond generic discrimination against states controlled by Democrats and, in at least one case, organized a multipronged attack on a single state because its criminal justice system prosecuted, convicted, and refused to release a Trump backer and election denier. Following the 2020 election, Tina Peters, the clerk and recorder of Mesa County, Colorado, was convicted of multiple felonies for breaching the county election systems in a supposed effort to prove that the election had been rigged and lying about it thereafter. In October 2024, Peters was sentenced to nine years in prison. In the years since 2020, Peters had become a prominent figure in the election-denialist circles supporting Trump, persisting in unsubstantiated claims that somehow Trump had been cheated out of the election against Joe Biden. When Peters was sentenced, Trump declared himself outraged. In August 2025, he demanded that Colorado release Peters, threatening that if it did not, “I am going to take harsh measures.” On Dec. 11, 2025, Trump issued what he characterized as a pardon of Peters. The president’s pardon power does not extend to state crimes. At the same time Trump was issuing his counterfeit pardon, he was reportedly fuming behind the scenes. On Dec. 15, 2025, White House special assistant Emily Underwood sent an email to five federal agencies organizing an urgent “brainstorm call” in order to, in her words, “prioritize Colorado for the purposes of ensuring grants and federal support are in line with administration priorities.” The next day, the Departments of Transportation and Energy, which had been invited to the “brainstorm,” canceled hundreds of millions of dollars in federal grants for projects in Colorado. In addition to the cuts in transportation and energy projects, two days after the “brainstorm” call, the Trump administration ordered that Boulder’s National Center for Atmospheric Research, a world leader in climate and earth systems research, be dismantled, shuttering some programs and moving others out of state. In late December, Trump denied two Colorado disaster relief requests stemming from wildfires and a flood. Also in December, the Department of Agriculture attacked Colorado’s federally supported Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program by imposing new requirements that would have been impossible to meet. On Dec. 30, 2025, Trump used his presidential veto to kill a congressionally authorized water project for the Eastern Plains region of Colorado. If there were any doubt about the connection between all these actions and Trump’s fury over the Peters prosecution, Trump removed them with a New Year’s Eve social media post in which he said, of Colorado Gov. Jared Polis and the Republican district attorney who prosecuted Peters: “I wish them only the worst,” and they should “rot in Hell.” Trump’s assault on Colorado was, if anything, more constitutionally egregious than his general attacks on other Democratic states. In his actions against other states, Trump and his minions were simply giving rein to general political animus or, in some cases, selectively applying his peculiar policy fixations, such as his hatred of clean energy. In Colorado, Trump employed the might of the federal government to coerce a state into granting undeserved personal relief to one properly convicted state felon. Worse, the felon in question was a figurehead in Trump’s ongoing effort to destroy confidence in the nation’s electoral system. Worst of all, the pressure worked: Polis granted Peters clemency in May and she was released in June, after having served less than a quarter of her original sentence. This was as blatant, and impeachable, an abuse of presidential power as can well be imagined. Multiple courts have entered orders blocking parts of Trump’s funding and program cancellations in states led by Democrats. In particular, in January 2026, a federal judge enjoined the assault on Colorado’s SNAP requirements. The move to dismantle NCAR was enjoined by the same federal court in June 2026; the court determined that the government’s action was procedurally “arbitrary and capricious” and that “retaliation played at least some role.” However, the question of whether presidential action is impeachable does not turn on the judgments of courts, particularly when the essential ground for an article of impeachment is the president’s active subversion of the basic constitutional design. It is absolutely plain that the president may not consciously discriminate against a state government or the residents of a state based purely on the partisan political alignment of the state’s residents or its government. Nor can a president enlist the power of the federal government to coerce a state’s justice system into granting special treatment to a personal favorite of the president, particularly if that favorite’s crime was committed in aid of the president’s own attack on the nation’s electoral system. Considered together, Trump’s partisan attacks on Democratic states and their residents amount to an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment.
